### Phase 6 — Calibration and polish

**Build**
- a projection backtest on 2025, comparing Sleeper's 2025 weekly projections (same endpoint, season 2025) with nflverse weekly actuals, to tune α and σ. If the 2025 projections aren't available, tune σ from actuals only and report that α couldn't be tuned.
- whether K and DEF get a season-to-date weight back, from that backtest (0 since Checkpoint 7e: planner reply to Checkpoint 7d, item 1.2, "Phase 6's backtest decides whether to bring the weight back"; Section 6 step 1, `config.projections.actualWeightByPos`)
- estimates of the injury model's `M_pos` and d from nflverse weekly data (2018–2025) for fantasy-relevant players, replacing the defaults (Section 6)
- a negative binomial for rush attempts, completions and receptions, with the measured dispersion (TDs stay Poisson), and the stat SDs refit on the archived pre-game projection snapshots (Sections 2.3 and 5)
- live game-day scores, so the odds count points already scored in the week in progress (Checkpoint 3 answer 6)
- ESPN's documented seed-by-seed procedure for ties among 3+ teams (Checkpoint 3 answer 7, low priority)
- the acceptance sanity check against real Sleeper trades
- the platforms' trade review periods (Checkpoint 4: a trade made before kickoff counts the current week in full today)
- which version was sent (offer first, full trade, Plan B): analyze the feedback records (Checkpoint 4 answer 8)
- a backtest of `marketBlend` on the archived FantasyCalc lists (Section 2.9) and the pre-game projection snapshots (Section 2.3)
- base-rate stability (Checkpoint 4b): one projection refresh moved Parker's playoff odds by 10 points. Measure day-to-day odds swings that injuries and results don't explain, and smooth the base rate if they're large (for example, average the last 2–3 pre-game projections)
- OUT players into open IR slots (Checkpoint 4b answer 8)
- other teams' pickups modeled with ω₀ (planner reply to Checkpoint 7b, §4: "so a user's goal never changes how other teams behave"): today one ω, the user's, sets every team's pickups, chips and claims (Section 7), so under "Several prizes" (Parker's ω 1.06 against 1.98 under "Only the champion") other teams' modeled pickups can move with his setting
- three model behaviors the odds analysis of Checkpoint 7d found (`reports/checkpoint-7d.md`, question 2; Parker, 2026-10-14: "yes, add the three model behaviors to the Phase 6 list"), each worth a point or two of a team's odds on Parker's week 5 data:
  - the Q/D/P base rate can raise a projection: it reads a questionable player's nearest later healthy week first and sets this week's shape to 1 (Section 6 step 3), so one whose next week projects above his usual level goes up (3 of the 32 rostered questionable players; DJ Moore 7.4 → 9.4 expected points in week 5, his tag alone −1.4 points of Parker's playoff odds)
  - a tagged player doesn't shape his position's market-blend curve (Section 6 step 8), so his tag moves other players' factors (with Ja'Marr Chase questionable, Puka Nacua reads 17.15 points a game instead of 16.45; +1.8 points of Parker's playoff odds)
  - the ESPN/Sleeper averaging test (within 10% on average, Section 6 step 1) is a knife edge: the league read 10.03%, and one team's pickup or week of lines takes it under 10% and switches ESPN's averaging on for about 285–296 base rates at once (−0.2 to +0.5 points of Parker's odds); for example, fade the averaging in over a band around the bar, or decide it per player
- a second dynasty value source: DynastyProcess for dynasty leagues (a median after normalizing), only after a license review (it is GPL-3.0)
- outcome feedback and the refit script
- error and loading states
- accessibility
- performance work

**Then:** Checkpoint 6.

### Phase 7 — Public launch, only when Parker asks

(Pulled ahead as Phases 7a and 7b above, planner reply to Checkpoint 4c; this list stays the reference for what launch needs.)

**Hosting**
- Use Vercel's free Hobby plan. It only allows non-commercial use: ads or payments would break its terms, but asking for donations doesn't.
- A placeholder landing page is already live on Parker's Vercel project, `offersheet`. Deploy the app to **that same project** so the web address doesn't change, and move the landing page's copy into the app's home page.
- A custom domain is optional. offersheet.app was unregistered on 2026-09-29.

**Add before going public**
- a privacy policy at `/privacy` and terms of use at `/terms`
- environment variables and the session secret in Vercel
- a shared cache store and per-IP rate limits on server routes
- error logging that never records credentials or league data
- installable PWA support: a manifest and icons
- a feedback link
- the server-side feedback store: anonymized, rate-limited, with an opt-out (Section 11)
- the Offer Sheet Chrome extension for ESPN cookies (Section 14)
- an email to FantasyCalc before launch (Section 2.9), which also asks them to confirm the te+/te++ meaning (done: they answered on 2026-10-05, confirming the mapping, with conditions for free use)
- basic SEO: title, description and an Open Graph image
- an attribution check: FantasyCalc everywhere values show (since their conditions, a link in the server HTML of every page), and Yahoo everywhere Yahoo data shows
- the DynastyProcess ID map is GPL-3.0: keep it server-side, credit DynastyProcess, and review the license before any downloadable or app-store version (Checkpoint 4b, change 12)

**Load test**
- 50 league imports at the same time, without hitting any platform's rate limits.

**Don't deploy before Parker asks.**

---

## 18. Checkpoint report format

```
# Checkpoint N — <phase name>
Status: ✅ Done | ⚠️ Done with issues | ❌ Blocked

## What works
- plain-English bullets

## What doesn't / open issues
- issue — evidence (URL without secrets, HTTP status, JSON path) — options

## Decisions I made
- decision — why

## Numbers
- tests passing; checklist ✅/⚠️/❌ counts; runtimes; match rates

## Questions (numbered, each answerable in one line)
1. …

## How Parker can try it
- exact commands / URL
```

- Keep it under about 150 lines.
- No secrets.
- No real names of leaguemates. Use placeholders such as "Team 3".

---

## 19. Terms, attribution and privacy

- **The plan is a free public app, and it must stay non-commercial.** Ads, payments or paywalls would break:
  - Vercel Hobby's terms (non-commercial only)
  - Sleeper's API terms (non-commercial only)
  - FantasyCalc's terms (non-commercial only)

  They'd also make ESPN's unofficial API a bigger risk. Don't add any of them unless Parker and the planner decide to, and get the licenses first.
- **ESPN:** the API is unofficial and can change or break at any time. Keep it isolated behind its adapter, and fail loudly with a clear message.
- **Yahoo:** follow the API Access and Use Agreement and the attribution rules. Read-only.
- **FantasyCalc** (its conditions for free use, 2026-10-05: `reports/fantasycalc-permission.md`, Section 2.9; until then: a link on every page that shows values, and at most one fetch a day per format): non-commercial, and free while under about 10,000 sessions a month (`npm run usage:report -- --live` warns from 8,000, so Parker can write to them first); call only `/values/current`, each format at most once every 3 hours and only when someone needs it; a link back to fantasycalc.com on every page that shows anything derived from their values, at least one in plain server-rendered HTML (the footer), with `rel="noopener"` and `referrerpolicy="origin"`; and never display their values: no value numbers and no value comparisons anywhere in the UI.
- **DynastyProcess:** the player ID map (Section 2.9) is GPL-3.0. It stays on the server, the app credits DynastyProcess, and its license is reviewed before any downloadable or app-store version (Phase 7). A saved import keeps its players' ID pairs, DynastyProcess's links among them, in the user's browser (`reports/security-review.md`, finding 10), so since Checkpoint 7d the license is credited where users can see it (planner reply to Checkpoint 7c, "Also"): the footer's "DynastyProcess" links to its data repository, https://github.com/dynastyprocess/data, and the terms' data line reads "player ID maps from DynastyProcess (shared under the GPL-3.0 license)" (Section 14). The full GPL review still comes before any downloadable version.
- **Saved leagues** (Phase 4c): imports, picks, settings, model inputs and feedback stay in the user's own browser (IndexedDB) until removed; the privacy page says so. Credentials are never stored.
- **Credentials:** ESPN cookies and Yahoo tokens only ever go to their own platform. Never log them, and never store them on the server. Never ask for ESPN passwords.
- **Feedback data (Phase 7):** store only the anonymized fields in Section 11. Say so in the UI and the privacy policy, and offer an opt-out. (Built at Checkpoint 7a: Section 11.)
- **The beta's providers** (Checkpoint 7a): Vercel hosts the app (Hobby: non-commercial; request logs kept 1 hour); Upstash (through Vercel's Marketplace, US East; the privacy page also says backups are off, ⚠️ checked by Parker in Upstash's console) holds the server cache (encrypted), the rate-limit counts, the request budgets, the error log and the anonymous feedback, and since Phase 7b the public-data archive (Sleeper's pre-game projections and FantasyCalc's daily lists, and since Checkpoint 7c each day's rest-of-season levels from Sleeper's own projections in its standard scoring formats, never per league, as the privacy page says; up to 2 seasons, about no league or person beyond which formats were in use each day) and the usage counters (daily totals with no identifiers, about 13 months). The privacy page names both and says what each holds and for how long; it also names Google, which keeps the emails people send to Offer Sheet's Gmail inbox. A visitor's browser reads public ESPN and Sleeper leagues itself (those platforms see the visitor's IP address and which league), without cookies.
- **Deploying:** previews through `npm run preview:deploy -- --live` only (protected; Section 16). Production deploys only through `npm run prod:deploy -- --live --checkpoint <the approved tag>`, and only after Parker writes "go" (the exception the planner made in the reply to Checkpoint 7b, §2; Section 16): the script runs `vercel deploy --prod` itself, from a clean copy of that tag, and nothing else may. `vercel --prod` (or `--target production`), `promote`, `alias` and `redeploy` stay forbidden, and so do `--public`, and `vercel link` and `vercel env pull` in this folder (both write into .env.local). Putting the previous production deployment back is Instant Rollback in the dashboard, or `vercel rollback` run from another folder: Parker's call (the steps: `rollbackSteps`, printed by every live run). Checking production again after a deploy is `npm run prod:deploy -- --verify --checkpoint <the tag it serves>` (no Vercel CLI, no deploy), never a second `--live`: Hobby's Instant Rollback reaches only the previous production deployment, so a second deploy would leave no way back to the landing page. `.vercelignore` must stay the allowlist.
  - Production, only on Parker's "go" (launch day, Tuesday, October 13, 2026; launched on Wednesday, October 14, 2026: `checkpoint-7d`, Section 17's Phase 7d). On launch day, before the go: its own `SESSION_SECRET` (`npm run vercel:secret -- --live --env production`) and `CRON_SECRET` (`npm run vercel:secret -- --live --name CRON_SECRET`: production is its default, since Vercel runs crons on production deployments only; without it the cron route answers 401 and saves nothing), the three WAF rules below, in their order (published: the dry run already asks the image optimizer's paths and the cross-site rule for their 403s), the Next check, then the dry run, `npm run prod:deploy -- --checkpoint <the launch tag>` (every line ✅; it names the rollback target, the landing page's deployment); after the go, `npm run prod:deploy -- --live --checkpoint <the launch tag>`, then the phone check. The launch tag: `checkpoint-7d`, approved on 2026-10-14 (planner reply to Checkpoint 7c: "If 7d is approved in time, production deploys `checkpoint-7d` instead"; it holds the 7b follow-ups and the pages' "Last updated" of October 13, which no change since has moved). `vercel.json` already schedules the cron. The steps, in order, with the rollback: `reports/beta-infrastructure.md`, "Launch-day steps".
  - One Vercel WAF rate-limit rule on `/api/*` ("API rate limit", second in the list, below the cross-site rule), set at launch: by IP, 80 requests per 10 minutes (Checkpoint 7a's "about 60–80", confirmed at Checkpoint 7c, planner reply to Checkpoint 7b, §4: above every route's own limit, import 30, model 30, session 20, health 12 and cron 10 per 10 minutes and feedback 60 an hour, so a user who overuses one route gets the app's own answer first; a normal session makes 3–8 `/api` requests; `reports/beta-infrastructure.md`), over the limit Too Many Requests (429) only: never Deny (a 403, which the app shows as "Something went wrong on our side", and a browser read whose upload gets it tries again through the server; review of the follow-ups, DOC-1) and never a challenge: a challenge sets Vercel's own cookie (`_vcrcs`), against the privacy page's "Offer Sheet sets no cookies, except one encrypted cookie if you connect a private ESPN league." ⚠️ Per Vercel's docs Hobby offers one such rule (a fixed window of 10 s to 10 minutes, keyed by IP or JA4); were it missing, the app's own limits stand alone (Section 3). (Protected previews also set Vercel's own `_vercel_jwt`: `reports/privacy-terms-draft.md`.)
  - In the same step (Parker, 2026-10-07), a WAF custom rule that denies Vercel's image optimizer: request path starts with `/_next/image` OR starts with `/_vercel/image`, action Deny (403, never a challenge). The app doesn't use it, and Vercel still answers both paths despite `images.unoptimized` (the 7b preview, 2026-10-07: `?url=/apple-icon.png&w=32` gave the same 348-byte PNG on both paths and `w=64` a 605-byte one on `/_vercel/image`; `w=33` on `/_next/image` and `icon.svg` on `/_vercel/image` got 400; on 2026-10-06 other sites' images got 400 on `/_next/image`). "Block image optimizer", third in the list. Launch-day steps: `reports/beta-infrastructure.md`, "Launch-day steps".
  - In the same step, first in the list (required since the planner's reply to Checkpoint 7c, answer 4: "deny `/api/` requests whose `Sec-Fetch-Site` header is `cross-site`. Put it before the rate-limit rule so those requests don't count toward a visitor's limit"; `reports/security-review.md`, finding 15): "Deny cross-site API requests", request path starts with `/api/` AND request header `sec-fetch-site` equals `cross-site` → Deny. The firewall's rate limit counts what the app refuses before counting (BAD_ORIGIN, Section 3), so without it a page on another site could use up a visitor's 80. The order, from Vercel's docs (read 2026-10-09): "Deny: Blocks the request and no further rules are evaluated." (Rule Configuration Reference, updated 2025-04-21) and "You also have the ability to re-order the precedence of each custom rule." (WAF Custom Rules, updated 2026-09-10): a Deny above the rate limit ends the request before the rate limit sees it (✅ the docs; ⚠️ not tried live: only a visitor past 80 requests could show it). Hobby: "Hobby projects can have up to 3 total custom firewall rules" (WAF Rate Limiting, updated 2026-08-28), the rate limit among them, so the three rules use them all; if Vercel refuses a third anyway, the cross-site condition becomes a third OR group of "Block image optimizer", which then sits above the rate limit. Current browsers send `Sec-Fetch-Site` (⚠️ an old one that sends none passes and is counted, as before the rule); the cron, `vercel curl` and the scripts send none, and the app's pages ask `/api` same-origin. Phase 5 (review of Checkpoint 7d, PD7D-3): an `/api/` address another site sends the browser to arrives `cross-site`, so this rule and `withRoute`'s same-origin check (BAD_ORIGIN) both refuse it: Yahoo's OAuth callback (Section 2.4, `/api/auth/yahoo/callback`), and perhaps the Chrome extension's requests (⚠️ unchecked), each need an exception in both or an address outside `/api/`; the rule lives only in Vercel's dashboard, where no test sees it. The launch-day check ("a request with that header gets 403, and the app still works"): `npm run prod:deploy` asks `/api/health` with `Sec-Fetch-Site: cross-site` (the firewall's 403, never the app's own BAD_ORIGIN 403) and with `same-origin` (not denied), and `/` opened from another site (not denied: a link on another site opens a page with `Sec-Fetch-Site: cross-site` too, so a rule without its `/api/` path condition would deny every page reached from another site; review of the policy-deploy track, PD-1), in the dry run, before a live upload, after it and with `--verify` (Section 16).

---

## 20. Out of scope for the MVP

- sending trade offers, or making roster moves on any platform
- accounts and payments
- other sports
- AI-generated text (templates only for now)
- keeper-cost modeling (the UI says it isn't modeled)
